Message 
From the Municipal Planning and Development Coordinator 
The Sangguniang Bayan, led by Vice Mayor Leonardo C. 
Anunciacion and including Hon. Levy M. Tadeo, Mary Andrei 
Lacsamana, Hon. Semel Manipon, Hon. Rainier Rivera, Hon. Jayson 
Galang, Hon. Marino Rafael M. Morales, Hon. Alvin Sibal, Hon. Robin 
Mangiliman, IPMRJunJun Tolentino, and SK Federation Jake Casper 
Maglaqui 
Our Municipal Local Government Operations Officer 
(MLGOO), Mr. Romer T. Enaje 
DepEd Superintendents Ms. Loida L. Zamora and Mr. Roberto D. 
Santos 
The Bureau of Fire (FS Insp. Cedric T. Crnz) 
Bamban Chief of Police Leoncio P. Alcantara,Jr. 
The Civil Society Organizations, led by People's Council President 
Mr. Arnaiz S. Salas, Ms. Minerva Lumboy, Ms. Dianne Figueroa, 
former Vice Mayor Fred Lumboy, Mr. Louie Mendiola, Ms. Elena 
Salas, Mr. Roland Canlas, and all members of the People's Council 
The CLUP 2022-2031 will guide Bamban toward sustaining its land and water 
resources, preserving ecological integrity, and promoting balanced 
development. It positions Bamban as an emerging municipality poised for 
infrastructure growth and economic progress. More than a plan, it is a blueprint 
for a vibrant, resilient, and inclusive community-one that nurtures 
development while safeguarding the environment for generations to come.

The completion of the 2022-2031 Comprehensive Land Use Plan would not have been possible without the invaluable support, assistance, and contributions of various 
government and non-government agencies. The Municipal Government of Bamban extends its deepest gratitude to the following:
The Municipal Government of Bamban also extends its heartfelt appreciation to the President of the Bamban People’s Council, Mr. Arnaiz S. Salas and his team of Civil Society 
Organizations and to non-governmental organizations whose invaluable input and contributions during consultations greatly aided the crafting of the CLUP document. Equal 
gratitude is given to the various divisions and ofﬁces within the city government for generously sharing their time, data, and expertise.

Definition of Terms 
Consumer Price Index 
Indicator of the change in the average prices of a fixed basket of goods and services commonly purchased by households relative to a 
base year force. (PSA) 
 
 
Employment Rate 
Percentage of the total number of employed persons to the total number of persons in the labor force. (PSA) 
 
 
Food threshold 
The minimum income/expenditure required for a family/individual to meet the basic food needs, which satisfies the nutritional 
requirements for economically necessary and socially desirable physical activities. (PSA) 
 
 
Income Gap 
The average income shortfall, expressed in proportion to the poverty threshold, of families/individuals with income below the 
poverty threshold. (PSA) 
 
 
Inflation Rate 
The annual rate of change or the year-on-year change in the Consumer Price Index. (PSA) 
 
 
Labor Force 
Participation Rate 
Percentage of the total number of persons in the labor force to the total population 15 years old and over. (PSA) 
 
 
Labor Productivity 
Measured as the ratio of Gross Domestic Product to total employment (national estimate) or the ratio of Gross Value Added to 
industry employment (industry estimate). (PSA) 
 
 
Poverty Gap 
The total income/ expenditure shortfall (expressed in proportion to the poverty threshold) of families/ individuals with income/ 
expenditure below the poverty threshold, divided by the total number of families/ individuals. (PSA) 
 
 
Poverty Incidence 
The proportion of families/individuals with per capita income/expenditure less than the per capita poverty threshold to the total 
number of families/individuals. (PSA) 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan​​

===== PAGE 26 =====
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan 
 
 
 
Poverty Threshold 
The minimum income/expenditure required for a family/individual to meet the basic food and non-food requirements. (PSA) 
 
 
Severity of Poverty 
The total of the squared income/expenditure shortfall (expressed in proportion to the poverty threshold) of families/ individuals 
with income/expenditure below the poverty threshold, divided by the total number of families/ individuals. (PSA) 
 
 
Subsistence Incidence 
The proportion of families/individuals with per capita income/expenditure less than the per capita food threshold to the total 
number of families/ individuals. (PSA) 
 
 
Underemployment 
Rate 
Percentage of the total number of underemployed persons to the total number of employed persons. (PSA) 
 
 
Unemployment Rate 
Percentage of the total number of unemployed persons to the total number of persons in the labor force. (PSA)

1.​ Profile 
1.1.​ Municipal Profile 
   Table 1.1. Bamban Profile 
Region 
III 
Province 
Tarlac 
Income Class 
Second Class 
Boundaries 
North: Municipality of Capas, Province of Tarlac 
East: Municipality of Concepcion, Province of Tarlac 
South: City of Mabalacat, Province of Pampanga 
West: Municipality of Botolan, Province of Zambales 
South West: Municipality of Porac, Province of Pampanga 
No. of Barangays 
15 
 
The municipality of Bamban is a second-class municipality in the Province of Tarlac. It is located in Central Luzon, bounded on the north by the municipality of 
Capas (Tarlac) on the east by the municipality of Concepcion (Tarlac), on the south by the municipality of Mabalacat (Pampanga), on the west by the 
municipality Botolan (Zambales), and on the southwest by the municipality of Porac (Pampanga).  
 
The municipality is politically subdivided intro 15 barangays, namely: Anupul, Banaba, Bangcu, Culubasa, Dela Cruz, La Paz, Lourdes, Malonzo, San Nicolas, 
San Pedro, San Rafael, San Roque, San Vicente, Santo Niño, and Virgen de los Remedios

1.2.​ History 
The history of the municipality of Bamban from its institution as a municipality during the pre-Hispanic Times to the impact of the historical eruption of Mt. 
Pinatubo in 1991 is summarized on the table below.  
 
Table 1.2. List of Historical Events 
Year 
Event 
Pre-Hispanic 
Times 
Institution of Bamban  
1700 
Spanish Colonization Period and the spread of Roman Catholic Faith 
1876 
Establishment of Mission de Pueblos de Bamban 
Don Martin Sibal was appointed head and commissioned with the rank of Captain 
1894 
The integration of Bamban into Tarlac from Pampanga 
1896 
Revolution from Spanish conquerors 
1899 
Filipino-American revolution and proclamation of Bamban as the country’s revolutionary capital by General Emilio Aguinaldo. In recognition of this 
historical event, the Sangguniang Bayan of Bamban passed a special ordinance in 1999- the centennial anniversary of the proclamation-declaring June 6 as 
Bamban Day. 
1942 
Japanese soldiers took over the sugar central and converted it as their barracks or detention camp. 
Establishment of (KALIBAPI) Kapisanan ng Panglilingkod sa Bagong Pilipinas to gain the cooperation and good will of civilians. 
Bambanenses joined guerilla movements such as the United States Armed Forces in the Far East (USAFFE) or the Hukbong Bayan Laban sa Hapon 
(HUKBALAHAP) 
Apung Nasiong Gamboa (also known as Commander Luna) lead the #45 of HUKBALAHAP 
1944 
Liberation and Post-Liberation Years 
The entire poblacion was devastated when the American fighter planes bombed houses, school buildings, and the public market.  
The aftermath of the war lingered for an extended period, casting a shadow over the town. This prolonged period of uncertainty and instability prompted 
influential families to make a significant decision: relocating to the city in order to rebuild their lives and businesses. The allure of the city's resources, 
opportunities, and relative safety likely played a pivotal role in their decision-making process. This migration of prominent families further altered the social 
and economic landscape of both the town and the city, marking a significant transition in the wake of the war's devastation. 
1949 
 Atty. Igmedio Bolus established the first secondary school in Bamban, “Bamban Institute”

Year 
Event 
1957 
The Holy Infant Jesus Academy was founded by Mr. Gaviola, however the management was then transferred to the Dominican Sisters and renamed the 
school into Sto. Niño Academy. 
1966 
Bamban’s First Public High School was established “The San Roque Experimental High School” now currently known as San Roque High School 
1972 
​
The detention of the Incumbent Mayor and subsequent replacement by the Vice Mayor caused significant disruption in the municipality's leadership. This 
abrupt change led to instability, uncertainty, and a loss of confidence among residents. The Vice Mayor faced challenges transitioning into the role of Mayor 
and struggled to fill the void left by their predecessor. This period of adjustment affected decision-making, policy implementation, and public trust. The 
absence of the Incumbent Mayor, who likely had strong community ties, resulted in a lack of leadership experience and political influence. Overall, the 
fallout highlighted the importance of stable leadership in local government for maintaining governance and stability. 
1991 
Mt. Pinatubo Eruption and Expiration of RP-US Bases Treaty 
 
the eruption of Mt. Pinatubo in 1991 devastated Bamban, affecting its economic, social, cultural, and political life. Lahar caused widespread destruction, 
submerging entire barangays and displacing residents. Two resettlement areas, Dap Dap and Mainang, were established to accommodate those affected. 
Schools were built in these areas to cater to the relocated children. 
 
The closure of the Clark Air Base due to the expiration of the RP-US Military Bases Treaty in the same year added to the economic woes, leaving many 
unemployed. However, the National Government later transformed the former military base into a special economic zone under the Clark Development 
Corporation. This led to the establishment of manufacturing plants, duty-free shops, and other businesses, creating employment opportunities for Bamban 
residents. Additionally, the Clark International Airport was developed into a major international gateway, further bolstering the local economy.

1.2.1.​
Municipality of Bamban: Governance, Development Collaborations, and Strategic Initiatives 
The Municipality of Bamban, Tarlac, continues to navigate governance challenges, including boundary disputes with Capas, Concepcion, and Mabalacat 
City. Despite these disputes, the local government remains committed to providing essential services and fostering development within its jurisdiction. 
Through strategic inter-LGU collaboration, partnerships with national agencies, and innovative local governance initiatives, Bamban has positioned 
itself as a key player in regional progress. 
 
1.2.1.1.​
Inter-LGU and Inter-Governmental Collaborations 
Bamban actively engages in inter-local and inter-governmental partnerships to address regional concerns, infrastructure development, and economic 
growth. Key collaborations include: 
1.2.1.1.1.​ Inter-LGU Cooperation 
Metro Clark Advisory Council (MCAC) – Bamban collaborates with other LGUs within Metro Clark, including Capas, Mabalacat City, 
and Angeles City, to align policies and infrastructure development with the greater Clark Freeport Zone.​
 
Tarlac Inter-LGU Development Initiatives – The municipality coordinates with neighboring towns and the provincial government 
of Tarlac on initiatives such as disaster resilience, tourism promotion, and agricultural productivity. 
1.2.1.1.2.​ Inter-Governmental Engagements 
Collaboration with Clark Development Corporation (CDC) & Bases Conversion Development Authority (BCDA) – Bamban 
benefits from its proximity to Clark Freeport Zone and New Clark City, with ongoing infrastructure development, job creation, and 
investment incentives.​
 
Joint Management Agreement (JMA) for CADT-025A – A collaborative framework involving the Ayta Mag-antsi Indigenous 
Peoples (IPs), CDC, and the National Commission on Indigenous Peoples (NCIP), ensuring the sustainable management of 10,600 
hectares of ancestral land within the Clark Economic Zone.​

National Programs Participation – Bamban has actively participated in Gawad Kalasag, Child-Friendly Local Governance, and the 
Seal of Good Local Governance (SGLG), which led to the acquisition of four rescue vehicles for enhanced disaster response. 
1.2.1.2.​
Local Institutional and Governance Platforms 
 
Bamban has adopted various governance innovations to enhance public service delivery, economic growth, and community engagement. 
 
1.2.1.2.1.​ Governance and Development Platforms 
 
Municipal Development Council (MDC) – Serves as the primary policy-making body for local development planning and investment 
prioritization.​
 
Barangay Development Planning Program – Strengthens community-driven development by ensuring each barangay aligns its projects 
with municipal and national priorities.​
 
Local Economic and Investment Promotion Office (LEIPO) – Encourages local and foreign investments by promoting Bamban’s 
strategic location near Clark Freeport and New Clark City.​
 
Public-Private Partnerships (PPP) – Encourages private sector collaboration in infrastructure projects, such as road expansion, market 
development, and renewable energy initiatives. 
 
1.2.1.2.2.​ Innovative Local Governance Programs 
 
Bamban Disaster Resilience Program – Involves climate change adaptation projects, early warning systems, and community-based 
disaster risk reduction programs.​
 
E-Governance and Digitalization – Introduction of digital services for business permits, online tax payments, and GIS-based 
urban planning to improve efficiency and transparency.​

Sustainable Livelihood and Skills Training Programs – In partnership with TESDA and the Department of Labor and Employment

Indigenous Peoples Development Plan – Ensures the protection and inclusion of Ayta Mag-antsi communities in local governance, 
education, and economic programs. 
 
1.2.1.3.​
Economic and Infrastructure Development 
 
Bamban, being strategically located between two major economic zones (Clark Freeport Zone and New Clark City), benefits from national and regional 
development efforts. 
 
1.2.1.3.1.​Infrastructure Projects 
Connectivity & Road Networks – Development of Capas-Botolan Road and other access routes to improve mobility and trade.​
 
Water, Power & Telecommunications – Expansion of utility services from New Clark City and Metro Clark, ensuring sustainable resource 
distribution.​
 
National Government Administrative Center (NGAC) – A government hub in NCC that decentralizes Metro Manila offices, improving 
accessibility for Bamban residents. 
 
 
1.2.1.3.2.​Investment and Business Growth 
Entrepreneurship and MSME Development – Support for local businesses, agribusiness, and tourism through investment incentives and 
training programs.​
 
Green and High-Tech Industries – Opportunities for eco-friendly businesses, logistics hubs, and smart technology enterprises in alignment 
with New Clark City’s sustainability framework 
1.2.1.4.​
Addressing Socio-Cultural and Environmental Considerations

1.2.2.​
Historical and Cultural Heritage Areas 
Bamban is home to several historical and cultural heritage areas that reflect its rich past, indigenous traditions, and important role in national history. While it 
does not have UNESCO-listed World Heritage Sites, Bamban's local heritage is deeply rooted in both its indigenous culture and historical events, particularly 
from the colonial period and World War II. 
 
Table 1.2.1. List of Historical and Cultural Heritage Areas 
Cultural Heritage Areas 
Description 
Location 
Bamban WWII Museum 
The Bamban Museum of History is a key cultural site that showcases the town’s 
historical evolution. It highlights important aspects of Bamban’s past, including the 
indigenous Aeta community who have lived in the area for centuries, and artifacts from 
the Spanish and American colonial periods. The museum serves as a repository of the 
town's collective memory, aiming to preserve and promote awareness of Bamban's local 
heritage. 
Banaba 
Aeta Ancestral Lands 
The Aeta people, one of the Philippines' earliest indigenous groups, have ancestral lands 
in the mountainous regions of Bamban, particularly near Mount Pinatubo. These lands 
are significant not only for their natural beauty but also for their cultural importance, 
representing the traditional lifestyle, customs, and spiritual connection of the Aetas to 
their environment. Their presence is integral to Bamban’s cultural identity, and their 
ancestral domains are recognized as valuable both historically and culturally. 
Anupul, Lourdes, San 
Nicolas, San Roque, San 
Vicente, Santo Niño 
World War II Historical Sites  
Bamban played an important role during World War II, with various strategic military 
sites in the area. Remnants of Japanese tunnels and old military camps from the 
occupation era can still be found, which were used by both the Japanese forces and 
Filipino resistance fighters. These sites are of historical significance, serving as reminders 
Lourdes, San Nicolas, San 
Vicente

of Bamban’s involvement in the broader conflict and its contributions to the war effort. 
They provide a historical link to the town’s resilience during this challenging period in 
Philippine history. 
Old Bamban Bridge 
The Old Bamban Bridge, which spans the Bamban River, is an important historical 
landmark. It holds significance both for its role in local infrastructure and its strategic 
importance during World War II. The bridge was a vital crossing point during the war, 
and its legacy is still remembered in local historical accounts. 
Lourdes 
Religious and Colonial Heritage 
Bamban is home to significant religious structures, such as the San Nicolas de Tolentino 
Parish Church. This church and other religious sites reflect the town’s deep-rooted 
Catholic traditions, a legacy of the Spanish colonial period. These religious landmarks 
are integral to the town's cultural fabric, playing key roles in community life and 
historical development over the centuries.

1.3.​ Geographic Location 
Bamban, classified as a second-class municipality, is one of 17 towns in Tarlac Province, with coordinates at latitude 15.2373082 and longitude 120.4703258. It is 
regarded as a medium-sized town by the provincial government and is positioned in the southernmost area of the province, about 100 kilometers north of Manila 
and 32 kilometers from the provincial capital, Tarlac City. Bamban shares its borders with Capas to the north, Concepcion to the east, Mabalacat to the south, 
and Botolan in Zambales to the west. The Sacobia River, marking its southern boundary, is a major waterway susceptible to lahar and pyroclastic flows from Mt. 
Pinatubo. 
 
1.4.​ Territorial Jurisdiction and Barangay Subdivision 
Bamban is part of the Third Congressional District of Tarlac. It is composed of 15 barangays, totaling 34,705.64 hectares, which is the municipality's designated 
official land area. The barangays of Bamban are: Anupul, Banaba, Bangcu, Culubasa, Dela Cruz, La Paz, Lourdes, Malonzo, San Nicolas, San Pedro, San Rafael, 
San Roque, San Vicente, Santo Niño, Lumang Kalsada, and Virgen de los Remedios. 
 
There are six barangays which are considered urban barangays, and 9 rural barangays. The urban barangays are: Anupul, Dela Cruz, Lourdes, San Nicolas, San Roque, 
and Virgen de los Remedios.

1.5.​ Demographic Profile 
1.5.1.​
 Population 
Based on the 2020 PSA Census, Bamban's total population was recorded at 78,260. This represents a consistent increase, with an annual population growth rate 
(APGR) of 2.41%, calculated from the 2015 Census figures, and 2.29% based on the 2010 Census. The rising growth rate suggests significant demographic shifts 
over the past decade, influenced by factors such as migration, urban development, and natural population increase. These trends reflect the evolving dynamics of 
Bamban's population and underscore the importance of careful planning to accommodate future growth. However, the 2022 Community-Based Monitoring 
System (CBMS) recorded a significant decrease in Bamban's population, dropping to 74,292, primarily due to the effects of the COVID-19 pandemic. The 
pandemic led to increased mortality rates, economic hardship, and migration as residents sought safer or more stable living conditions. These factors, along with 
disruptions in birth rates and local development, contributed to the population decline. The sharp contrast between the town's earlier growth and the 
post-pandemic reduction highlights the substantial impact the health crisis had on Bamban’s demographics.

1.5.2.​
Population Density 
Based on the latest 2020 PSA Census, the population density for the Municipality of Bamban is 311 persons per square kilometer or 3.11 persons per hectare. 
The population density was calculated using the total population and the land area as of December 2013 which was based on LMB and DENR and as certified by 
DBM. Compared to the figures in 2020, there was a 25.4% increase from population density of 2010 and 12.7% increase from 2015 figures.  The population 
density of Bamban is generally lower than the average population density of the province of Tarlac.

1.5.4.​
Labor Force and Dependent Population 
Based on the 2020 Census, the productive population of the municipality of Bamban was recorded at about 63.59 % of the total population. The total 
dependency ratio is at 36.41%. This is comprised of 32.03% young-dependency ratio and 4.39% old-age dependency ratio.  
 
While on the 2022 CBMS results, Bamban's productive population, or labor force, accounts for approximately 66.73% of the total population, equating to 
49,578 individuals. Meanwhile, the dependent population represents 33.27% of the total population.

1.6.​ Physical Features and Environmental Conditions 
1.6.1.​
 Topography 
1.6.1.1.​
Topographic Relief 
Bamban is situated at the southernmost tip of Tarlac Province. It is bordered to the north by Capas, to the east by Concepcion, to the south by Mabalacat City in 
Pampanga, and to the west by Botolan in Zambales. The town lies approximately 100 kilometers north of Manila and 32 kilometers from Tarlac City. 
 
1.6.1.2.​
 Slope 
Bamban’s topography is characterized by extreme slope variation, from level to mountainous slopes. 25.72% of total land area have hilly to mountainous terrain, 
23.41% have mountainous slopes, and 21.96% have level to undulating hills.  
 
Table 1.6.1.2. Slope Distribution in Bamban

1.6.1.3.​
Elevation 
The  Municipality of Bamban, exhibits a pronounced elevation gradient, with its terrain ranging from 48m to 1,475m at its highest elevations. The municipality’s 
low-lying plains, characterized by gently sloping, flat terrain, are optimally suited for both agriculture and human settlement, owing to their fertile soils and ease 
of cultivation. 
 
Progressing westward, the landscape ascends into the foothills of the Zambales Mountain Range, ushering in more rugged and mountainous  topography. These 
upland zones are distinguished by cooler microclimates and dense forest cover. Critically, they serve as essential components of the local ecosystem - functioning 
as vital watersheds, sustaining regional biodiversity, and contributing significantly to watershed management for rivers such as the Sacobia-Bamban.
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan​​

1.6.1.4.​
Surface Drainage 
Bamban is traversed by two major rivers and their tributaries. The Sacobia River, located in the southern part of the municipality, is the primary river system, with 
its headwaters originating from Mt. Pinatubo. Similarly, the headwaters of the O'Donnell River System, which also flows from Mt. Pinatubo are found within 
Bamban. Since much of the municipality's land is classified as forest, it is also home to several small creeks that occasionally provide irrigation for agricultural 
areas.

1.6.3.​
Soil Characteristics 
Majority total land area or 30.99% (10,753.67 has.)  of the area of Bamban have Angeles soil. This is followed by Tarlac clay loam with 18.72% (6,497.22has.) and 
Tarlac soil with 17.75% (6,160.84 ha). 
 
1.6.3.1.​
Soil Suitability 
Bamban's soils are well-suited for growing a variety of crops, including rice, corn, mango, pineapple, vegetables, sugarcane, yam, and oil palm. The barangays of 
Anupul, San Vicente, and Santo Niño have soils ideal for cultivating a wide range of crops, including cacao, garlic, and legumes. In contrast, the barangays of 
Bangcu and Malonzo are the least favorable areas for crop production.

1.6.4.​
Climate 
Bamban has two (2) pronounced seasons, the wet season that starts from May up to lasts up to October and dry from the month of November to April. This type 
of climate is typically hot, humid, and tropical and is generally affected by the neighboring topography and prevalent wind direction that varies within the year. 
Tropical monsoon is carried into the area from the southeast in the month of May to September thereby causing heavy rainfall in the area. Most of the rainfalls 
are associated with typhoons. 
 
1.6.4.1.​
Temperature 
The average temperature in Bamban from 2010 to 2020 ranged from 25ºC (Dec. 2011) to 31ºC (May 2021). The highest recorded temperature was 37ºC (May 
2021) and the lowest recorded temperature was 19º C (Jan. 2014) 
 
Figure 1-1. Max, min and average temperature in Bamban, 2010-2020

1.6.4.4.​
Air Pressure 
Atmospheric or air pressure is equivalent to the weight of air above a given area on Earth’s surface or within its atmosphere. It is usually expressed in millibars 
(mb) or kilopascal (kPa). At sea level, the mean pressure is about 1,000 mb (100 kPa), varying by less than 5 percent from this value at any given location or time 
(Britannica, 2021). 
 
Over the last decade, the lowest atmospheric pressure recorded in Bamban was at 1,006.3 millibars (July 2018 and August 2019) and the highest at 1,016 millibars 
(February 2019).  
 
Figure 1-5. Average air pressure in Bamban 
 
 
Source: https://www.worldweatheronline.com/bamban-weather-averages/tarlac/ph.aspx

1.6.4.5.​
Cloudiness 
Cloudiness or cloud cover refers to the extent to which the atmosphere is covered by cloud and is estimated in fractions or percent. Overcast refers to near 100% 
cloudiness while clear refers to near 0% cloudiness. The term cloudiness most often describes the horizontal extent of clouds, including all cloud altitudes as 
viewed from a weather station on the Earth or a satellite in space. 
 
From 2010 to 2020, the period with the least cloud cover in Bamban was in November 2015 with only 9 percent cloudiness. The period with the most cloud 
cover was in August 2012 with 62 percent. 
 
Figure 1-7. Average cloud coverage in Bamban, 2010 – 2020 
 
Source: https://www.worldweatheronline.com/bamban-weather-averages/tarlac/ph.aspx

1.6.4.6.​
Humidity 
Humidity is the amount of water vapor in the air. It is the most variable characteristic of the atmosphere and constitute a major factor in climate and weather 
(Britannica, 2013). 
 
The highest relative humidity of 90 percent was recorded in the municipality of Bamban in September 2019. The lowest average was at 57 percent which 
happened in May 2018. 
 
Figure 1-8. Average humidity in Bamban, 2010 – 2020

1.6.4.7.​
UV Index 
The UV Index measures the strength of ultraviolet (UV) radiation from the sun, indicating the potential risk of skin and eye damage. The scale ranges from 0 
(low risk) to 11+ (extreme risk), with higher values signaling greater danger and the need for more protection, such as sunscreen and protective clothing. The 
index is highest around midday and helps raise awareness about sun safety. 
 
Figure 1-9. UV radiation exposure category 
 
Source: https://www.who.int/uv/publications/en/UVIGuide.pdf 
 
 
 
 
Based on the 2010 to 2020 data, the UV Index in Bamban ranges from 6 to 7.  Based on the monthly average UV index of Bamban, the average UV index from 
November to June is at 7 and is at 6 for the rest of the year. Thus, comparing this to the WHO standards, as shown above, it falls under the ‘High’ exposure 
category. 
 
 
 
 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan​​

1.6.5.​
Geological/Environmental Hazard Areas 
In Bamban, Tarlac, areas more likely to experience noise and air pollution typically include: 
Areas Prone to Noise Pollution: 
●​ Main Roads and Highways: Areas near major thoroughfares like national highways often experience higher traffic noise. 
●​ Commercial Centers: Areas with shops, restaurants, and markets can generate significant noise from both customers and delivery vehicles. 
●​ Construction Zones: Sites undergoing construction or renovation are typically noisier, affecting nearby residents. 
●​ Industrial Zones: Proximity to factories or industrial operations can lead to elevated noise levels from machinery and equipment. 
Areas Prone to Air Pollution: 
●​ Traffic-Congested Areas: Regions with heavy vehicle traffic, particularly near intersections and transport hubs, are likely to have poorer air quality. 
●​ Industrial Areas: Areas near factories or manufacturing plants can experience higher levels of air pollutants from emissions. 
●​ Agricultural Zones: Regions where intensive farming practices are common may have increased air pollution due to the use of fertilizers and pesticides, 
as well as burning of agricultural waste. 
●​ Residential Areas Near Waste Disposal Sites: Locations close to landfills or open burning sites may experience air quality issues from waste 
decomposition and burning. 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan​​

1.6.6.​
 Groundwater Resources 
The data in table 1-16 shows that most areas in Bamban have local and less productive aquifers, meaning groundwater availability is limited. Anupul and San 
Vicente are the largest areas with these low-yield aquifers, covering over 7,000 hectares each. Some parts, especially in disputed areas and Anupul, also have rocks 
with low groundwater potential. Smaller barangays like La Paz and Banaba have minimal areas with such aquifers. Overall, Bamban's groundwater resources 
appear to be limited across much of its territory. 
 
Table 1.6.6. Groundwater Resources 
Description 
Area (per ha)

32,374.46

1,958.04 
Rocks without any known significant groundwater obtainable through drilled wells largely, untested 
373.15

1.6.7.​
Conservation Areas and Other Special Interest Areas 
1.6.7.1.​
Prime Agricultural Land 
Bamban's 3,352.00 hectares of agricultural land are ideal for cultivating rice, sugarcane, and corn due to the area's fertile soil and favorable tropical climate. Rice 
requires ample water, while sugarcane and corn are more drought-tolerant, with sugarcane thriving in well-drained soils and corn being adaptable to various 
conditions. This diverse crop mix supports both food production and economic growth, making efficient use of the region's agricultural resources. 
 
1.6.7.2.​
Primary Forest and Other Forest Types 
Of the total area, 15,288.50 hectares is classified as public forest. This comprises an extensive military reservation and forestlands on the western sections of the 
Municipality and the Sacobia Resettlement Area. The vast area under the Sacobia Development Authority includes the lands under the jurisdiction of Barangays 
San Vicente and Sto. Niño, notwithstanding the boundary dispute between Bamban and the City of Mabalacat. Likewise, about 10,684 hectares out of 
15,288.50 hectares now fall under Certificate of Ancestral Domain Title (CADT) No. Ro3 BAM-12-4-025 of the Bamban Aetas (CLUP 2004). Also within the 
territorial jurisdiction of Bamban are Zone F and Zone C, which are areas returned to the Philippines after the expiration of the RP-US Military Bases Agreement 
of 1947. Three barangays, namely: Lourdes, Sto. Niño, and the mountains of San Vicente, lie within forestland protection areas. 
 
Table 1.6.7.2. Primary Forest and Other Forest Types

1.6.7.3.​
Critical Watershed 
Bamban is home to three significant watersheds. The largest is the Sacobia-Bamban River Watershed, covering 18,059.41 hectares, followed by the O'Donnell 
Watershed, which spans 11,263.04 hectares. The smallest is the Cut-Cut River Watershed, at 5,383.20 hectares. These watersheds are crucial for water resource 
management, supplying water to surrounding communities, agriculture, and ecosystems. The varying sizes highlight the region's diverse capacity for water 
collection, flood control, and resource sustainability. 
 
Table 1.6.7.3. Critical Watersheds 
Critical Watersheds 
Area

Source: NAMRIA 
Note: The land area figures used in this table are based on the results of GIS mapping and are strictly for land use planning purposes only. 
 
1.6.7.4.​
Mining and Quarrying Areas 
The Municipality of Bamban is home to a registered rock crushing plant operated by Jafcor Development Company Inc., which plays a vital role in supporting 
the region’s infrastructure and development projects. This facility processes large rocks into smaller, usable construction materials—such as sand, gravel, and 
crushed stone—that are essential for building roads, drainage systems, buildings, and other civil works. The eruption of Mt. Pinatubo has significantly 
contributed to the availability of volcanic ash, much of which is now deposited in the Parua River. The river flows through the barangays of Malonzo, San Pedro, 
Lourdes, Santo Niño, and San Pedro, with these volcanic ash deposits increasingly being utilized in construction activities, including embankment projects. 
The rock crushing plant operates by feeding large rocks into a primary crusher, which breaks them down into smaller fragments. These fragments are then 
subjected to further refinement through secondary and sometimes tertiary crushers. Once processed, the materials are sorted by vibrating screens and transported 
between stages via conveyor belts. The plant primarily produces coarse aggregates for concrete, fine sand for mortar, and larger stones for erosion control. To 
minimize its environmental impact, the plant is equipped with a control system that incorporates dust suppression mechanisms to reduce air pollution. 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan​​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
74 


===== PAGE 78 =====
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
   
 
All rock crushing plants in Bamban are required to comply with both local and national regulations. This includes securing permits and clearances from the 
barangay, Municipal Government, Provincial Environment and Natural Resources Office (PENRO), and an Environmental Compliance Certificate (ECC) from 
the Department of Environment and Natural Resources (DENR). Furthermore, the plant’s location must align with the zoning classifications outlined in the 
municipality’s Comprehensive Land Use Plan (CLUP), ensuring that these activities do not encroach upon residential areas, agricultural zones, or 
environmentally critical areas. The Municipal Environment and Natural Resources Office (MENRO) conducts regular monitoring and inspections to ensure 
adherence to environmental standards, particularly in managing dust, noise pollution, water usage, and land rehabilitation. 
The total area zoned for industrial use, including mining and quarrying activities in Bamban, spans approximately 472,600 square meters. In contrast, the total 
area where volcanic ash deposits are found is substantially larger, measuring approximately 27,135,600 square meters. This stark contrast highlights the significant 
need for effective oversight and sustainable practices to balance economic development with environmental protection and community welfare. While there have 
been reports of metallic minerals such as gold, manganese, copper, and chromite in the area, there is currently no comprehensive report on the actual production 
of these metallic minerals from the Environment and Natural Resources Office. 
This underscores the importance of continual monitoring and stringent regulatory practices to ensure the long-term sustainability of Bamban’s mining and 
quarrying operations, while safeguarding its natural resources for future generations. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan​​

The Bamban River is deemed polluted due to the elevated levels of fecal coliform bacteria, especially in August and December. These readings significantly 
surpass safe limits, indicating considerable contamination, likely from sewage or agricultural runoff. Although other parameters, such as biochemical oxygen 
demand and dissolved oxygen, are mostly within acceptable ranges, the high fecal coliform counts indicate that the water is unsafe for activities like swimming or 
drinking. It is essential to address this pollution to enhance the river's water quality.

1.6.9.​
Probable Source of Pollution  
A.​
Agricultural Activities: 
●​ Chemical Use: The use of fertilizers and pesticides in farming can lead to soil and water pollution, affecting both local ecosystems and human 
health. Proper management and regulation of these chemicals are essential to mitigate their impact. 
●​ Livestock Waste: Improper disposal of manure from livestock can contaminate water sources and soil, leading to environmental 
degradation and health risks. 
B.​
 Industrial Activities: 
●​ Small-Scale Manufacturing: Local industries may generate waste and emissions that contribute to air and water pollution. Implementing 
environmental regulations and waste management practices can help reduce these impacts. 
●​ Construction Activities: Dust and noise pollution from construction sites can affect air quality and community health. 
C.​
Transportation: 
●​ Vehicle Emissions: National and Provincial Highway traffic, as well from nearby highway such as the SCTEX, contributes to air pollution. 
Encouraging cleaner transportation options and improving traffic management can help mitigate this issue. 
●​ Infrastructure Development: Noise and dust from ongoing infrastructure projects can impact local air quality and community well-being. 
D.​
Domestic and Municipal Waste: 
●​ Untreated Sewage: Inadequate sewage systems can lead to water pollution, posing health risks to residents. Improving waste treatment facilities 
is crucial. 
●​ Improper Waste Disposal: Open dumping of household waste contributes to soil and air pollution, necessitating effective waste management 
strategies. 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan​​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
82 


===== PAGE 86 =====
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
   
 
E.​
Natural Sources: 
●​ Flooding: Natural flooding can carry pollutants into water sources, affecting water quality. Implementing flood control measures and 
maintaining natural buffers can help mitigate this risk. 
●​ Geologic Risks: Bamban's geology may pose risks such as landslides or soil instability, which can lead to environmental degradation if not 
properly managed. 
F.​
Infrastructure and Development Projects: 
●​ Construction Noise: Noise pollution from ongoing infrastructure projects can affect community health and quality of life. 
●​ Dust and Particulates: Air pollution from construction activities can exacerbate respiratory issues among residents.

1.6.10.2.​
Liquefaction 
Liquefaction is the phenomenon wherein sediments, especially near bodies of water, behave like liquid similar to a quicksand. Its effects include sinking and/ or 
tilting of structure above it; sand boil1; and fissuring2. Liquefaction can result from ground shaking due to the movement of fault-lines or volcanic eruption. All 
of the total land area of Bamban is highly susceptible to liquefaction. Table 1-16 below shows excerpts on liquefaction hazards from the CDRA.

1.6.10.5.​
Fault Lines 
Bamban is not located on any active fault lines, providing a relatively stable environment in terms of seismic activity. However, the nearest active fault, known as 
the Care Fault, is situated approximately 20.1 kilometers southwest of the municipality. While this distance offers some buffer against the direct impact of fault 
movements, the proximity of the Care Fault means that Bamban could still experience the effects of an earthquake, such as ground shaking, depending on the 
magnitude and nature of seismic events originating from the fault. This highlights the importance of earthquake preparedness in the region despite the absence of 
an active fault within the municipality itself.

The 1999-2004 Comprehensive Land Use Plan (CLUP) set ambitious goals for urban planning, economic development, infrastructure, agriculture, and 
social services in Bamban. While some progress was made, several areas remain unaddressed or require significant improvement. One of the key challenges 
identified is the lack of updates to the CLUP and zoning ordinance, leading to zoning conflicts and unregulated developments. Although employment 
opportunities increased, Bamban lagged behind neighboring municipalities in supporting small and medium-scale industries, limiting its economic growth 
potential. 
In the agricultural sector, fishpond production and irrigation expansion efforts were minimal, and modern agri-technology remains underutilized. Infrastructure 
investments were made during the planning period, but poor maintenance and monitoring have resulted in the deterioration of some facilities. Social services saw 
mixed results—while efforts to improve nutrition were successful, housing shortages persist, with around 15,000 families still in need of resettlement. 
Additionally, healthcare facilities, though available, suffer from a lack of manpower, and many training centers and educational support facilities have deteriorated 
over time. 
Peace and order efforts require reinforcement at the barangay level to ensure social stability. Furthermore, the failure to regularly update urban planning policies 
has slowed development and caused reclassification issues that complicate land use. To address these challenges, it is crucial to update the CLUP and zoning 
ordinance, strengthen infrastructure maintenance and funding for social services, expand SME support and agri-technology adoption, develop long-term housing 
solutions, and enhance barangay-level security programs. With proper planning and strategic policy updates, Bamban has the potential to become a regional 
economic hub while ensuring sustainable and inclusive growth for its residents. 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan​​

1.7.4.​
Residential 
Residential lands in Bamban cover 1,119.24 hectares or 3.22% of the total land area of the municipality. This type of land use is the fourth largest land use in the 
municipality. 
 
1.7.5.​
Commercial 
The existing commercial lands cover 219.81 hectares of 0.63% of the municipality. The commercial area of Bamban is concentrated in Barangays Anupul, 
Lourdes, and San Nicolas (Poblacion). While other commercial establishments can be found along major roads within the municipality. 
 
1.7.6.​
 Agri-Industrial 
Bamban is home to a diverse range of agricultural and livestock facilities, contributing significantly to its local economy and food production. The municipality hosts 
multiple poultry, piggery, and game farms spread across various barangays. In Santo Niño, the Renato Antonio Game Farm, AVM Gamefarm, and Erin Jay Farm - 
Poultry Products operate, while El Nikko Farm is located in San Vicente. San Rafael houses Primary Breeders Inc. - Atis Farm, a Poultry Farm, and Charlisons 
Farm Corp. In San Pedro, several agricultural enterprises, including 3G Piggery Farm, Apple Farm Poultry, Bounty Blueberry, QSeed Genetics, Inc., and 
Masaya Swine Farm Inc., contribute to livestock production. Additionally, Culubasa is home to Good Harvest Farm - Poultry, JRS Agri Farms Corporation, 
Jose R. Simeon Agri Farms Corporation, and First Statefarm Ventures, Inc. 
Anupul has a strong presence of piggery and poultry farms, including Brookside Group of Companies - Piggery, a general Piggery, Celevy Farm - Piggery, 
Boston Ridge Piggery Farm, Anupul Farm, and Poultrypure Farms Corporation along McArthur Highway. It is also the location of key processing and 
agricultural facilities such as the Slaughter House, EMC Rice Mill, and Bamban Poultry Dressing Plant. Similarly, Cathay Farms Development Incorporated, 
Madera Poultrex Layer Farm, White Feather Farm, QJJ Piggery Farm Inc., IBC Avocado Poultry Farm, Eduardo Desisto Semi Cono, Rowena Romero 
Cono & Mechanical Dryer, Diomedes Dizon Farm, and Bounty Fresh Food Inc. are located in Virgen de los Remedios. This area also features two Inoza Feedmill 
(Blue and Red) plants, a Corn Pavement Dryer, a Corn Mechanical Dryer, an Agri-Warehouse, and the Rowena Romero Grain Warehouse. 
Meanwhile, San Pedro accommodates Apollo Egg Science Tech Corporation, Inoza Agro Development Corp., and Celeca Agro Farm Inc. Bangcu is home to 
another Poultry Farm and Celeca Agro Farm Inc. Lastly, Bounty Farms, Inc. is found in San Rafael, adding to the poultry industry of the municipality. These 
facilities, scattered across Bamban, serve as vital contributors to the local food supply chain, supporting employment and economic growth while ensuring the steady 
production of poultry, swine, and other agricultural goods.

1.7.13.​
Manmade Structure 
The Bamban River Irrigation Diversion Dam, located along the Bamban River in Barangay San Nicolas, Bamban, Tarlac, covers an area of approximately 0.83 
hectares and serves as an important man-made structure for the municipality's agricultural sector. This dam plays a vital role in managing the water supply for 
irrigation, particularly benefiting the town's primary crops, such as rice, sugarcane, and corn. By regulating the flow of water from the river, the dam ensures a 
steady and reliable source of water for farmlands, supporting the productivity and livelihood of local farmers. While not a large-scale reservoir, it remains a crucial 
part of Bamban's infrastructure, contributing to the sustainability of the agricultural landscape. 
 
1.7.14.​
Ancestral Domain Overlay Zone 
The Ancestral Domain Overlay Zone in the Municipality of Bamban plays a critical role in preserving the integrity of its forest land use while ensuring the 
protection of the Indigenous Peoples (IPs) residing within these areas. This zoning designation is essential in regulating development, maintaining ecological 
balance, and safeguarding the cultural heritage of IP communities. 
Purpose and Significance of the Ancestral Domain Overlay Zone 
1.​ Cultural and Social Protection: Unlike the “Unat” (a term often used to describe lowland settlers or non-IPs), many IPs are traditionally nomadic 
or practice shifting cultivation. This means they do not permanently settle in one location but move within their ancestral territories depending on their 
needs, seasons, and traditional land use practices. The overlay zone ensures that development in these areas does not disrupt their natural way of life or 
displace them from their ancestral lands.​
 
2.​ Environmental Conservation: Forest lands within the ancestral domain serve as vital ecosystems that provide biodiversity, carbon sequestration, 
and climate regulation. The overlay zone restricts activities that could lead to deforestation, illegal logging, or land conversion, which could 
degrade the environment and threaten the livelihood of IP communities who depend on the forests for sustenance and cultural practices.​
 
3.​ Sustainable Development and Land Management: The overlay zone allows for controlled development, meaning that while economic activities and 
infrastructure projects may be permitted, they must align with the principles of sustainability, cultural sensitivity, and indigenous rights. This 
prevents unchecked urbanization or industrialization that could negatively impact both the IPs and the natural environment.​
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan​​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
104 


===== PAGE 108 =====
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
   
 
4.​ Legal Recognition of Indigenous Land Rights: The recognition of an Ancestral Domain Overlay Zone aligns with national policies such as the 
Indigenous Peoples Rights Act (IPRA) of 1997, which upholds the rights of IPs to their ancestral lands. This ensures that any proposed 
developments within these zones must undergo Free, Prior, and Informed Consent (FPIC) processes, wherein the IPs must be consulted and their 
approval secured before any project is implemented.​
 
5.​ Preservation of Traditional Practices: The zone helps maintain IP customs, rituals, and traditions, which are deeply tied to their environment. 
Forest areas serve as spiritual and cultural landmarks where indigenous ceremonies, hunting, and foraging practices take place.​
 
Challenges and Considerations 
●​ Encroachment and Land Conflicts: Despite legal protections, land-grabbing, illegal logging, and infrastructure projects sometimes threaten 
ancestral domains. Strengthening enforcement mechanisms and providing support for IPs to assert their rights are necessary.​
 
●​ Balancing Development and Conservation: While economic growth is important, it must be inclusive and respectful of the indigenous way of life. 
Sustainable livelihoods, such as eco-tourism, agroforestry, and handicrafts, can be promoted as alternative sources of income.​
 
●​ Climate Change Resilience: Many IPs rely on natural resources for food, medicine, and shelter. Climate change impacts, such as extreme weather 
events, can disrupt their way of life. Adaptive strategies, such as community-based forest management and disaster preparedness, are needed. 
The Ancestral Domain Overlay Zone serves as a safeguard to balance conservation, indigenous rights, and controlled development in Bamban’s forest 
lands. By recognizing and protecting the IPs' nomadic traditions, the municipality can ensure that these communities continue to thrive without being 
displaced or losing their cultural identity. This approach not only preserves biodiversity but also fosters a harmonious coexistence between development 
and indigenous heritage, paving the way for a more equitable and sustainable future. 
 
1.7.15.​
Alienable and Disposable Land 
Based on the available data from the Municipal Planning and Development Office (MPDO), the Municipality of Bamban currently does not have any classified 
Alienable and Disposable (A&D) lands. This is a significant aspect of land classification, as A&D lands are those that are declared suitable for private ownership, 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan​​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
105 


===== PAGE 109 =====
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
   
 
meaning they can be legally alienated or disposed of by the government. These lands are typically available for sale, lease, or distribution to private individuals or 
entities, subject to government regulations and policies. 
 
Alienable and Disposable Lands refer to lands that the government has declared fit for private ownership. These lands are part of the public domain but have been 
surveyed and classified as available for transfer to private ownership. The classification is made by the government following a process that determines the land's 
suitability for human habitation, agriculture, or other uses. In the Philippines, the Department of Environment and Natural Resources (DENR), through its 
Land Management Bureau, is responsible for classifying land and issuing the necessary declarations. 
 
For a piece of land to be classified as A&D, it must meet certain criteria: 
 
●​ Surveyed and mapped: The land must be surveyed and officially mapped by the government. 
 
●​ Non-forest land: The land must not be classified as forest land or part of protected areas. 
 
●​ Suitable for human settlement or agriculture: The land should be suitable for agricultural use or human habitation, meaning it is not reserved for 
environmental protection or other special purposes. 
 
●​ Government’s discretion: The government decides which lands are fit for alienation based on national priorities, such as addressing land distribution 
through agrarian reform or supporting economic development. 
 
Current Land Classification in Bamban 
At present, most of the undeveloped lands in Bamban are classified as agricultural land. This means the majority of the land within the municipality is intended 
for agricultural purposes and cannot easily be converted into residential, commercial, or industrial uses without undergoing a formal land conversion process 
through the Department of Agrarian Reform (DAR) or Department of Agriculture (DA). 
 
The absence of A&D lands in Bamban implies that the municipality’s available land resources are primarily tied to agriculture. Given this, the local government 
must carefully consider how to balance agricultural development with potential urbanization and other types of growth in the future. 
 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan​​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​

===== PAGE 110 =====

​
​
​
​
​
​
   
 
Implications for Development and Urbanization 
 
The lack of A&D lands in Bamban poses several challenges and considerations for the municipality’s future development: 
 
●​ Limited Expansion for Residential and Commercial Development: Since the land is mostly classified for agricultural use, any expansion for urban 
development (such as residential areas, commercial centers, or industrial zones) would require formal land conversion processes. This is a lengthy and 
often contentious process, as land conversion can lead to changes in the land's legal status and purpose. 
 
●​ Pressure on Agricultural Lands: With urbanization pressures increasing across the Philippines, including in areas like Bamban, there is often a temptation 
to convert agricultural lands into residential or commercial areas. However, this conversion must be carefully managed to prevent the loss of valuable 
agricultural land that supports local food production and livelihoods. 
 
●​ Impact on Agrarian Reform Beneficiaries: Given the lack of A&D lands, the municipality must also be mindful of the potential impacts on agrarian 
reform beneficiaries (ARBs) who rely on agricultural land for their livelihoods. The government must ensure that policies and programs do not 
unintentionally disrupt land tenure security for ARBs. 
 
●​ Conservation of Agricultural Land: The absence of A&D lands in Bamban highlights the need for sustainable land management policies that protect 
agricultural resources from being prematurely converted to non-agricultural uses. These policies should focus on ensuring that agricultural land remains 
viable for farming, particularly for smallholder farmers. 
 
●​ Future Planning for Infrastructure Development: The lack of A&D lands also affects the municipality’s ability to develop new infrastructure or housing 
projects. The local government will need to consider land use planning strategies that balance agricultural needs with emerging urban requirements, 
ensuring a harmonious relationship between agriculture and development. 
 
 
To address these challenges that Comprehensive Land Use Plan of Bamban considered the following: 
 
●​ Land Use Planning and Zoning: A comprehensive land use plan that designates specific zones for agricultural, residential, commercial, and industrial 
development could help manage competing land needs. This plan could also safeguard agricultural areas from premature conversion while allowing for 
the orderly development of urban spaces. 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan​​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
107 


===== PAGE 111 =====
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
   
 
 
●​ Promotion of Sustainable Agriculture: Encouraging sustainable farming practices and introducing agricultural innovations can help ensure that 
Bamban’s agricultural land remains productive and resilient, thus reducing the pressure to convert it for other uses. 
 
●​ Review of Land Conversion Processes: If there is a need for more A&D lands in the future, Bamban should work closely with national agencies to 
understand the processes for land classification and conversion. This will ensure that any future development is well-planned and aligns with national and 
local priorities. 
 
●​ Support for Agrarian Reform Beneficiaries (ARBs): The municipality should continue supporting the empowerment of ARBs through programs that 
improve their access to land, capital, and training, ensuring that agrarian reform objectives are met without compromising their livelihoods.

1.8.2.​
Health  
1.8.2.1.​
Health Facilities 
The data shows that there is a shortage of health infrastructure in the area. Currently, there are 2 Rural Health Units (RHUs), but 4 are needed, and this 
demand will rise to 5 RHUs in the future. Similarly, there are 10 Barangay Health Stations, but the demand is for 15, with a future need for 20 stations. 
Meanwhile, the 7.10 hectares of cemetery space is sufficient, with no additional space required for both current and future needs. This indicates that while 
health services need significant expansion, the cemetery capacity is adequate.

1.8.4.​
Social Welfare and Nutrition 
There are 27 Early Childhood Care and Development (ECCD) Centers in the municipality.  
 
There are 6,203 registered Senior Citizens, 385 Persons with Disability and 3,597 Indigenous People in Bamban. The Municipality has 15 Senior Citizen Center, 
0 Persons with Disability (PWD) Center, and 1 Drug Rehabilitation Center in San Roque. 
 
By using the standard of 1 daycare facility per 500 families, the required number of ECCDs for the municipality by 2031 is 45 which is currently met by the 
existing facilities.

1.8.5.​
Protective Services 
There are 69 PNP personnel, 225 BPATs, and 17 BFP fire marshals serving the people of Bamban. The local police station is located in Barangay Anupul.

1.8.8.​
Waste Management 
The municipality has an organized solid waste management system, collecting waste from all barangays three times a week and transporting residual waste to 
Metro Clark for final disposal. Each barangay has access to a Materials Recovery Facility (MRF), with the Municipal MRF located in Phase 1 of the Dapdap 
Resettlement area in Barangay Banaba, covering 10,356 square meters. The municipality operates two garbage compactors to efficiently handle the average daily 
solid waste volume of 26.69 cubic meters, promoting effective waste management and environmental sustainability.

1.9.​ Economic Structure 
Economic activities are traditionally classified into three sectors: primary, secondary, and tertiary. Activities falling under each sector that are present in the 
municipality are discussed below. 
 
1.9.1.​
Primary 
 
The Primary sector involves the extraction and production of natural resources. It includes activities related to agriculture, forestry, fishing, and mining.

1.10.3.​
Power, Water and Communication Network 
1.10.3.1.​
Power 
The Tarlac Electric Cooperative II (TARELCO II) is responsible for providing electricity to the municipality of Bamban. According to the energization status 
report from TARELCO I as of September 2019, 100% of the 15 barangays in Bamban have been fully energized. This means that all barangays in the 
municipality have access to electricity, reflecting a high level of electrification in the area, ensuring reliable power supply to support residential, commercial, and 
agricultural activities across Bamban. 
 
1.10.3.2.​
Water 
Only about 56.93% of the households are served by Level III water distribution systems, while the rest rely on deep wells and other water sources. 
 
Table 1.10.3.2. Household Data on Water Source

1.10.3.3.​
Communication 
Communication services in the municipality are served by telecommunication and postal service providers. The Philippine Postal Corporation, the national 
postal carrier of the Philippines continues to operate a mail station in the municipal building.  
 
The Philippine Long Distance Telephone Company provides wired telephone services while Smart, Globe, and DITO Telecommunity provide wireless 
telephone services.  
 
Approximately 98% of the total households in the locality owns information equipment like radio, TV, computers, cellular phones and other communication 
apparatuses. The municipality receives radio signals from radio stations in the province and neighboring provinces. There is also access to local TV networks and 
cable subscription is also an option. 
 
1.10.4.​
Designated Evacuation Centers 
There are 5 designated evacuation centers in Bamban. These evacuation centers have a total capacity of 880 individuals or 225 households. The table below shows 
the capacity and location of these evacuation centers.

1.11.​ ​
Institutional Sector 
1.11.1.​
Organizational Structure 
As provided under the Local Government Code (LGC) of the Philippines, each LGU has two (2) distinct branches of government, the Executive and Legislative 
Branches. 
 
Under Book III, Chapter 2, Section 443 of the LGC, Government., there shall be in each municipality a municipal mayor, a municipal vice-mayor, Sangguniang 
Bayan members, a secretary to the Sangguniang Bayan, a municipal treasurer, a municipal assessor, a municipal accountant, a municipal budget officer, a 
municipal planning and development coordinator, a municipal engineer/building official, a municipal health officer and a municipal civil registrar. 
 
In addition to the officials mentioned above, the mayor may also appoint a municipal administrator, a municipal legal officer, a municipal agriculturist, a 
municipal environment and natural resources officer, a municipal social welfare and development officer, a municipal architect, and a municipal information 
officer.

1.13.​ Development Constraints 
1.13.1.​
New Clark City (Military Reservation – converted into an Economic Zone, Managed by BCDA) 
Bamban is part of the Metro Clark Area, a region that has undergone significant transformation from a military reservation into a major economic hub. This 
area, managed by the Bases Conversion and Development Authority (BCDA), is central to the development plans for New Clark City, which aims to become a 
sustainable, world-class urban center. 
Approximately 3,980 hectares of New Clark City falls within the jurisdiction of the Municipality of Bamban, making it a crucial geographic and economic part 
of the municipality. However, since the area is managed by the BCDA as part of the economic zone, Bamban’s control over this portion is limited. This means 
that while Bamban’s local government may still have a role in providing services and maintaining some governance functions, major decisions related to land 
use, development policies, investments, and zoning in the area are largely influenced by BCDA’s strategic planning. 
This limited jurisdiction presents both challenges and opportunities for Bamban. On one hand, the municipality may struggle with autonomy and have limited 
input in the large-scale developments happening within its borders. It could face challenges in addressing local concerns, such as environmental sustainability, 
traffic management, and social services, in an area where control is shared or limited by the BCDA. On the other hand, the development of New Clark City 
could bring significant economic benefits, including jobs, infrastructure improvements, and increased revenue for Bamban. Yet, to fully leverage these 
advantages, Bamban must navigate its reduced authority within this economic zone, balancing collaboration with BCDA and advocating for the municipality’s 
interests. 
In essence, Bamban's position as part of the Metro Clark Area, particularly New Clark City, places it in a unique situation where its potential for growth is 
substantial, but its governance role is constrained by overarching economic zone policies and management. 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan​​

1.13.2.​
CADT-025A  
(on-going Joint Management Agreement talks between Clark Development Corporation and Aeta Magantsi Association Council) 
 
Background on the Indigenous Peoples Rights Act (IPRA) and the CADT-025-A Joint Management Agreement 
In 1997, the Republic of the Philippines passed into law Republic Act 8371, known as the Indigenous Peoples Rights Act (IPRA). This legislation is a milestone 
in recognizing, protecting, and promoting the rights of Indigenous Peoples (IPs), who make up approximately 18% of the national population. The IPRA is 
considered a landmark law for social justice and human rights, and it enshrines several key provisions to uplift and empower IP communities. These include: 
1.​ Securing land titles for IPs, safeguarding their ancestral domains and lands. 
2.​ Empowering IPs to manage their ecosystems and resources for their sustenance. 
3.​ Building capacity for self-governance within IP communities. 
4.​ Mainstreaming government services to ensure accessibility to IP communities. 
5.​ Preserving indigenous knowledge, culture, and traditions for future generations. 
To implement these mandates, the National Commission on Indigenous Peoples (NCIP), an independent government agency under the Office of the President, 
was created and entrusted with overseeing the enforcement of IPRA. 
Types of Land Titles Under IPRA 
IPs are eligible to apply for two types of land titles under IPRA: 
●​ Certificate of Ancestral Domain Title (CADT): A title that formally recognizes the rights of possession and ownership of IPs over their ancestral 
domains, which include lands, inland waters, coastal areas, and natural resources. These areas are identified and delineated under the terms of the law. 
●​ Certificate of Ancestral Land Title (CALT): A title that formally recognizes the rights of IPs over their ancestral lands, specifically lands occupied and 
utilized by individuals, families, or clans within Indigenous Cultural Communities (ICCs). 
 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan​​
​
​
​
​
​
​

​

===== PAGE 158 =====

1.13.2.1.​
Defining Ancestral Domains and Lands 
●​ Ancestral Domains are areas generally belonging to ICCs or IPs, including lands, bodies of water, natural resources, hunting grounds, burial sites, and 
worship areas. These domains are claimed under ownership by ICCs/IPs, either through individual or communal use, passed down since time 
immemorial. Section 56 of IPRA includes lands that may no longer be exclusively occupied by IPs but from which they have traditionally sourced their 
subsistence. 
●​ Ancestral Lands refer to areas individually owned by IP families or clans, continuously occupied and utilized since time immemorial. These lands 
include residential lots, rice terraces, private forests, and tree lots. The ownership of these lands is protected, except in cases of disruption caused by war, 
force majeure, or government-initiated projects. 
1.13.2.2.​
Joint Management Agreement for CADT-025-A 
In 2007, the NCIP, the Ayta Mag-antsi, and the Clark Development Corporation entered into a Joint Management Agreement for CADT-025-A. This 
agreement aimed to collaboratively manage, develop, and protect more than 10,600 hectares of ancestral domain, an area with overlapping territorial jurisdiction 
between the Ayta Mag-antsi community and Clark Development Corporation. However, the implementation of this agreement has been fraught with 
controversy, causing divisions and disunity among the Ayta Mag-antsi community members. 
1.13.2.3.​
Conflict Resolution and Recent Developments 
In a significant turn of events, the leaders of the Ayta Mag-antsi community united to document their Indigenous Peoples (Pinalupuyan Mamuun CADT 
024-A) and form a new Indigenous Peoples Organization (IPO), the Ayta Mag-antsi ng CADT 025-A. On September 14, 2022, the NCIP commission 
confirmed their IPs, marking a historic step toward conflict-sensitive ancestral domain management and conflict transformation. 
Following this, on September 22, 2022, the Ayta Mag-antsi IPs and their IPO, together with NCIP Chairman Allen Capuyan, engaged the Senate Committee on 
Cultural Communities and Muslim Affairs. The committee, led by Senator Robin Padilla through Senate Resolution No. 149, initiated an investigation into 
the issues surrounding the Joint Management Agreement. The committee vowed to fully support the IPs' struggle for self-determined development and 
sustainable management of their ancestral lands. 
 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan​​
​

1.13.2.4.​
Involvement of the Local Government Unit (LGU) of Bamban 
The Local Government Unit of Bamban was invited to be part of the Technical Working Group to address the issues related to the Joint Management 
Agreement. However, since the invitation, there has been a lack of updates provided to the LGU regarding the progress and outcome of the discussions. 
Additionally, during the planning phase of the Comprehensive Master Plan, the entire CADT area was designated as a forest zone, without taking into account 
the need for more nuanced land use planning or the concerns of the Ayta Mag-antsi community. 
1.13.3.​
Irrigation Lines 
 
The municipality of Bamban has two existing irrigation systems, both of which were constructed on privately owned land. These systems utilize surface 
irrigation, a method that relies on gravity to distribute water across the agricultural areas. However, changes in the river's water level have prevented water from 
entering the distribution system, resulting in the irrigation systems being only partially operational. Additionally, many of the lands adjacent to these irrigation 
systems are designated for protected agriculture, further emphasizing the need for a functional and reliable water supply. 
The municipality of Bamban has two existing irrigation systems, however these systems were built under private land. The existing system in the municipality is 
“surface irrigation” , the type of system that uses gravity to distribute water across the irrigated areas of the municipality. However due to the change of water level 
among the river, the water can no longer enter the distribution system. Currently, the irrigation system in the municipality is not fully functioning. However, 
most of the lands adjacent to these areas are under protection agriculture, therefore limiting the type of developments in the area. 
 
1.13.4.​
Flood Susceptibility 
A total of 4,070.79 hectares, or 11.72% of the total land area of Bamban, is classified as highly susceptible to flooding. This significant portion of the 
municipality’s land area faces frequent flood risks, particularly during heavy rains and typhoons. As a result, this susceptibility imposes considerable limitations 
on the types of developments that can be undertaken in these flood-prone areas. 
For instance, high-density residential or commercial developments may not be feasible due to the increased risks of property damage and safety hazards. Instead, 
flood mitigation measures such as proper drainage systems, flood barriers, or retention basins would be essential prerequisites for any development in these zones. 
Moreover, the local government must carefully regulate land use in these areas, potentially restricting development to agriculture, parks, or other non-structural 
uses that can better withstand flooding. 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan​​

​
​
   
 
These limitations require that urban planning efforts in Bamban focus on mitigating flood risks while balancing economic development and environmental 
protection. 
1.13.5.​
Landslide Susceptibility 
 
Approximately 15,933.67 hectares, or 45.09% of Bamban's total land area, are classified as highly to very highly susceptible to landslides. These vulnerable areas 
are primarily situated in the municipality's hilly and mountainous regions, where steep slopes, loose soil, and heavy rainfall during the monsoon season 
significantly increase the risk of landslides. 
This high susceptibility presents considerable challenges for development in these regions. Infrastructure projects, such as roads, residential housing, and public 
facilities, must be meticulously planned to avoid triggering landslides and to ensure the safety of residents. Comprehensive geotechnical assessments should be 
conducted before any construction activities, and engineering interventions—such as slope stabilization, retaining walls, and efficient drainage systems—are 
essential to reduce the risks posed by unstable terrain. 
Additionally, land-use regulations in these areas may need to emphasize conservation and environmental protection over intensive development. Activities such as 
agriculture, mining, and deforestation, which can destabilize slopes, should be closely monitored or restricted. The local government, in collaboration with 
relevant agencies, must also establish early warning systems and evacuation plans to protect residents, especially during typhoons and periods of heavy rainfall. 
Finding a balance between preserving the natural landscape and ecosystems while addressing the development needs of the population will be critical in 
mitigating landslide risks and ensuring the long-term safety and sustainability of Bamban's most vulnerable areas.

1.14.​ Comparative Advantages and Comparative Edge 
1.14.1.​
Potential Areas for Housing Projects 
The Municipality of Bamban is strategically positioned between two major economic zones in the Philippines: the New Clark City to its north and the Clark 
Freeport Zone, which includes the Clark International Airport, to its south. This central location presents Bamban with a unique opportunity to support and 
benefit from the growth of these economic hubs. However, one critical limitation of these zones is their inability to offer permanent housing solutions. 
Challenges of Housing in Economic Zones 
Both New Clark City and Clark Freeport Zone primarily operate on a long-term lease basis for their land and properties, limiting their capacity to develop 
permanent residential areas. These economic zones are designed to focus on industrial, commercial, and economic development, rather than large-scale residential 
housing. As a result, they provide only temporary or lease-based accommodations for workers, leaving a significant gap in the housing market for employees who 
seek stable, long-term living arrangements. 
Opportunities for Bamban 
This limitation creates a significant opportunity for the Municipality of Bamban to address the housing needs of current and future workers in these zones. As 
the demand for labor in these growing economic hubs increases, so will the need for accessible, permanent, and affordable housing. Bamban, due to its proximity, 
could develop into a residential haven that caters to the workers of these economic zones, offering: 
1.​ Permanent Housing Solutions: Bamban can focus on developing permanent housing facilities, including residential subdivisions and mixed-use 
communities, that provide long-term accommodations for workers and their families. 
2.​ Affordable Housing Options: With land in Bamban likely being more affordable than in the economic zones themselves, the municipality can attract 
developers to build cost-effective housing, making it an attractive option for workers at different income levels. 
3.​ Integrated Communities: Bamban has the potential to develop sustainable, integrated communities with essential services and amenities, such as schools, 
healthcare facilities, retail centers, and recreational areas, creating a high-quality living environment for workers and their families. 
4.​ Efficient Transport Links: By enhancing transportation infrastructure, Bamban can provide easy access to both economic zones, reducing commute times 
and improving the overall quality of life for workers. This could also attract businesses and services that cater to the growing population. 
 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan​​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​

Economic Impact for Bamban 
By addressing the housing gap, Bamban can position itself as a key player in the region's development. A thriving residential sector will bring economic growth 
through increased demand for local services, retail, and infrastructure. Moreover, the municipality can benefit from higher property taxes and revenues from new 
businesses, boosting its overall economic stability. 
In conclusion, the Municipality of Bamban holds the potential to transform into a residential hub that complements the rapid development of New Clark City 
and Clark Freeport Zone. By providing permanent housing solutions and creating integrated communities, Bamban can play a critical role in supporting the 
workforce of these economic zones while driving its own economic and social development. 
1.14.1.1.​
Socialized Housing in areas that are safer from floods and landslides (Areas of Potential) 
Several potential sites have been identified for development during this planning period, focusing on areas that are relatively safe from floods and landslides, and 
are no longer viable for crop cultivation. This strategic selection aims to maximize land use while minimizing environmental risks, ensuring sustainable 
development for the Municipality of Bamban. 
Criteria for Site Selection 
1.​ Flood Safety: The chosen sites are located in areas with a low susceptibility to flooding, which is critical for protecting infrastructure and ensuring the 
safety of residents. By avoiding flood-prone areas, the municipality can reduce the risk of property damage, loss of life, and costly disaster recovery efforts. 
2.​ Landslide Risk Mitigation: Identifying sites that are safe from landslides is equally important. These areas are typically located on flatter terrain or in 
stable geological zones, which minimizes the risk of erosion and soil instability. By prioritizing these locations, Bamban can foster a safer environment for 
residential, commercial, and industrial developments. 
3.​ Reduced Agricultural Viability: The selected sites have been assessed for their agricultural potential and deemed unsuitable for crop cultivation. Factors 
contributing to this classification may include soil degradation, contamination, or poor drainage. By utilizing land that is no longer viable for farming, the 
municipality can effectively repurpose these areas for urban development without displacing active agricultural practices.
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 1: The Land Use Plan​​
